From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Theory of human moral judgment

Dual process theory

within moral psychology is an influential theory of human

moral judgement

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral\_judgement

that posits that human beings possess two distinct cognitive subsystems that compete in

moral reasoning

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral\_reasoning

processes: one fast, intuitive and

emotionally-driven

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emotion

, the other slow, requiring conscious deliberation and a higher

cognitive load

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive\_load

. Initially proposed by

Joshua Greene

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joshua\_Greene\_(psychologist)

along with Brian Sommerville, Leigh Nystrom,

John Darley

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John\_M.\_Darley

Jonathan David Cohen

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan\_D.\_Cohen

and others,

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene,\_J.\_D.\_2001-1

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-pmid15473975-2

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-3

the theory can be seen as a domain-specific example of more general

dual process accounts

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychology

Daniel Kahneman

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel\_Kahneman

's "system1"/"system 2" distinction popularised in his book,

Thinking, Fast and Slow

. Greene has often emphasized the

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normative\_ethics

implications of the theory,

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-4

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2008-5

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2014-6

which has started an extensive debate in

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-7

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Singer\_2005-8

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Berker\_2009-9

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-10

The dual-process theory has had significant influence on research in

moral psychology

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral\_psychology

. The original fMRI investigation

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene,\_J.\_D.\_2001-1

proposing the dual process account has been cited in excess of 2000 scholarly articles, generating extensive use of similar methodology as well as criticism.

Figure 1: Schematic representation of Greene's dual processes model of moral judgement.This figure describes the processes underlying individuals' judgement about (a) the Trolley dilemma and (b) the Footbridge dilemma.

Core commitments

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=1

The dual-process theory of moral judgement asserts that moral decisions are the product of either one of two distinct mental processes.

automatic-emotional process

is fast and unconscious, which gives way to

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intuition

behaviours and judgments. The factors affecting moral judgment of this type may be consciously inaccessible.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-pmid17201791-11

conscious-controlled process

involves slow and deliberative reasoning. Moral judgments of this type are less influenced by the immediate emotional features of decision-making. Instead, they may draw from

general knowledge

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General\_knowledge

and abstract moral conceptions, combined with a more controlled analysis of situational features.

Greene conducted neuroscientific experiments where subjects were confronted with ethical dilemmas revolving around the famous

Philippa Foot

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippa\_Foot

Trolley Case

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trolley\_problem

(see Figure 1):

In the standard version, a runaway trolley is headed for five people who will be killed if it proceeds on its present course. The only way to save these people is to press a button that will turn the trolley onto a side track, where it will run over and kill one person instead of five. Surveys suggest the general consensus among both philosophers and lay persons is that it is morally acceptable to save five lives at the cost of one.

version of the problem, the setup is similar. As before, a runaway trolley threatens to kill five people, but this time the reader is standing next to a large stranger on a footbridge in-between the trolley and the five people. The only way to save the five people is to push the stranger off the bridge onto the tracks below. He will die, but his body will stop the trolley from reaching the five people. In this case, surveys suggest the consensus is that it is

okay to push a stranger to his death to save five lives.

To explain the differences in intuitions here,

Joshua Greene

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joshua\_Greene\_(psychologist)

claims that the two processes can be linked to two classes of ethical theories respectively.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2014-6

He calls this the

#### Central Tension Problem:

Moral judgments that can be characterised as

deontological

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deontological\_ethics

are preferentially supported by automatic-emotional processes and intuitions. Characteristically

utilitarian

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utilitarianism

judgments, on the other hand, seem to be supported by conscious-controlled processes and deliberative reasoning.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2014-6

The footbridge dilemma involves the thought of pushing someone to their death in an "up close and personal" manner, which activates a strong emotional response. Hitting a switch, by contrast, is much more impersonal and therefore does not evoke an emotional response. Greene hypothesies that this difference in emotional response explains why people respond so differently to these two cases. People tend toward consequentialism in the case where the emotional response is low and tend toward deontology in the case in which the emotional response is high.

Camera analogy

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=2

As an illustration of his dual-process theory of moral reasoning, Greene compares the dual-process in human brains to a

digital SLR

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital\_single-lens\_reflex\_camera

camera which operates in two complementary modes: automatic and manual.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2014-6

A photographer can either employ the automatic "point-and-shoot" setting, which is fast and highly efficient, or adjust and refine settings in manual mode, which gives the photographer greater flexibility.

Dual-process moral reasoning is an effective response to a similar

efficiency-flexibility trade-off

. We often rely on our "automatic settings" and allow intuitions to guide our behaviour and judgement. In "manual mode", judgments draw from both general knowledge about "how the world works" and explicit understanding of special situational features. The operations of this "manual mode" system requires effortful conscious deliberation.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2014-6

Greene concedes that his analogy has limited force. While a photographer can switch back-and-forth between automatic and manual mode, the automatic-intuitive processes of human reasoning are always active: conscious deliberations needs to "override" our intuitions. In addition to that, automatic settings of our brains are not necessarily "hard-wired", but can be changed through (cultural) learning.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2014-6

Interaction between systems

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=3

There is lack of agreement on whether and how the two processes interact with one another.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-12

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-13

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:2-14

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:4-15

It is unclear whether deontological responders, for example, rely blindly on the intuitively cued response without any thought of utilitarian considerations or whether they recognise the alternative utilitarian response but, on consideration, decide against it. These alternative interpretations point to different models of interaction: a serial (or "default-interventionist") model, and a parallel model.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:3-16

Serial models assume that there is initially an exclusive focus on the intuitive system to make judgements but that this default processing might be followed by deliberative processing at a later stage. Greene et al.'s model is usually placed within this category.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:2-14

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:4-15

In contrast, in a parallel model, it is assumed that both processes are simultaneously engaged from the beginning.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:3-16

Models of the former category lend support to the view that humans, in an effort to minimise cognitive effort, will choose to refrain from the more demanding deliberative system where possible. Only utilitarian responders will have opted into it. This further implies that deontological responders will not experience any conflict from the "utilitarian pull" of the dilemma: they have not engaged in the processing that gives rise to these considerations in the first place. In contrast, in a parallel model both utilitarian

deontological responders will have engaged both processing systems. Deontological responders

that they face conflicting responses, but they do not engage in deliberative processing to a sufficient extent to enable them to override the intuitive (deontological) response.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:3-16

Within generic

dual process

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory

research, some scientists have argued that serial and parallel models fail to capture the true nature of the interaction between dual process systems.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-17

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-18

They contend that some operations that are commonly said to belong to the deliberative system can in fact also be cued by the intuitive system,

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-19

and we need to think about

models in light of this evidence.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:4-15

Hybrid models would lend support to the notion of a "utilitarian intuition" - a utilitarian response cued by the automatic, "emotion-driven" cognitive system.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:4-15

Scientific evidence

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=4

Neuroimaging

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=5

A diagram depicting how Greene supposes the affective (automatic) response would override the consequentialist (manual) response in the footbridge case (below), but not in the switch case (above).

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:0-20

Greene uses

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Functional\_magnetic\_resonance\_imaging

to evaluate the brain activities and responses of people confronted with different variants of the famous

Trolley problem

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trolley\_problem

There are 2 versions of trolley problem. They are trolley driver dilemma and footbridge dilemma presented as follows.

The Switch Case "You are at the wheel of a runaway trolley quickly approaching a fork in the tracks. On the tracks extending to the left is a group of five railway workmen. On the tracks extending to the right is a single railway workman. If you do nothing the trolley will proceed to the left, causing the deaths of the five workmen. The only way to avoid the deaths of these workmen is to hit a switch on your dashboard that will cause the trolley to proceed to the right, causing the death of the single workman. Is it appropriate for you to hit the switch in order to avoid the deaths of the five workmen?

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Berker\_2009-9

(Most people judge that it is appropriate to hit the switch in this case.)

The Footbridge Case: "A runaway trolley is heading down the tracks toward five workmen who will be killed if the trolley proceeds on its present course. You are on a footbridge over the tracks, in between the approaching trolley and the five workmen. Next to you on this footbridge is a stranger who happens to be very large. The only way to save the lives of the five workmen is to push this stranger off the bridge and onto the tracks below where his large body will stop the trolley. The stranger will die if you do this, but the five workmen will be saved. Is it appropriate for you to push the stranger onto the tracks in order to save the five workmen?

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Berker\_2009-9

(Most people judge that it is not appropriate to push the stranger onto the tracks.)

A diagram showing the three main regions of the brain which Greene thinks are responsible for moral judgments.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:0-20

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-21

and his colleagues carried out fMRI experiments in order to investigate which regions of the brain were activated in subjects while responding to 'personal dilemmas' such as the footbridge dilemma and 'impersonal dilemmas' such as the switch dilemma. 'Personal dilemmas' were defined as any satisfying three conditions: a) The action in question could reasonably be expected to lead to bodily harm, b) The harm is inflicted in particular persons or members of a particular group and c) The harm is not a result of diverting a previously existing threat onto another party. All other dilemmas were classed as 'impersonal'. It was observed that when responding to personal dilemmas, the subjects displayed increased activity in regions of the brain associated with emotion (the medial

Prefrontal cortex

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prefrontal\_cortex

, the posterior

Cingulate cortex

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cingulate\_cortex

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precuneus

, the posterior

Superior temporal sulcus

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superior\_temporal\_sulcus

Inferior parietal lobule

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inferior\_parietal\_lobule

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amygdala

), while when they responded to impersonal dilemmas, they displayed increased activity in regions of the brain associated with

working memory

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working\_memory

Dorsolateral prefrontal cortex

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dorsolateral\_prefrontal\_cortex

Parietal lobe

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parietal\_lobe

). In recent work, Greene has stated that the

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amygdala

is primarily responsible for the emotional response, whilst the

Ventromedial prefrontal cortex

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ventromedial\_prefrontal\_cortex

is responsible for weighing up the consequentialist response against the emotional response. Thus, three brain regions are primarily implicated in the making of moral judgements.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:0-20

This gives way to what Greene

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2014-6

Central Tension Principle

: "Characteristically deontological judgements are preferentially supported by automatic emotional responses, while characteristically consequentialist judgments are preferentially supported by conscious reasoning and allied processes of cognitive control".

Greene points to a large body of evidence from

cognitive science

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive\_science

suggesting that inclination to deontological or consequentialist judgment depends on whether emotional-intuitive reactions or more calculated ones were involved in the judgment-making process.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2014-6

For example, encouraging deliberation or removing time pressure leads to an increase in consequentialist response. Being under cognitive load while making a moral judgement decreases consequentialist responses.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2015-22

In contrast, solving a difficult math problem before making a moral judgement (meant to make participants more skeptical of their intuitions) increases the number of consequentialist responses. When asked to explain or justify their responses, subjects preferentially chose consequentialist principles – even for explaining characteristically deontological responses. Further evidence shows that consequentialist responses to trolley-problem-like dilemmas are associated with deficits in emotional awareness in people with

alexithymia

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexithymia

or psychopathic tendencies.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2015-22

On the other hand, subjects being primed to be more emotional or empathetic give more characteristically deontological answers.

In addition, Greene's results show that some brain areas, such as the medial prefrontal cortex, the posterior cingulate/precuneus, the posterior superior temporal sulcus/inferior parietal lobe, and the amygdala, are associated with emotional processes. Subjects exhibited increased activity in these brain regions when presented with situations involving the use of personal force (e.g. the 'footbridge' case). The dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and the parietal lobe are 'cognitive' brain regions; subjects show increased activity in these two regions when presented with impersonal moral dilemmas.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2014-6

Arguments for the dual process theory relying on neuroimaging data have been criticized for their reliance on

reverse inference

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Functional\_magnetic\_resonance\_imaging#Reverse\_inference

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Poldrack2006-23

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Klein2010-24

Brain lesions

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=6

Neuropsychological

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neuropsychology

evidence from

lesion studies

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesion

focusing on patients with damage to the

ventromedial prefrontal cortex

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ventromedial\_prefrontal\_cortex

also points to a possible dissociation between emotional and rational decision processes. Damage to this area is typically associated with antisocial personality traits and impairments of moral decision making.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-pmid22136635-25

Patients with these lesions tend to show a more frequent endorsement of the "utilitarian" path in trolley problem dilemmas.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-pmid17377536-26

Greene et al. claim that this shows that when emotional information is removed through context or damage to brain regions necessary to render such information, the process associated with rational, controlled reasoning dominates decision making.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-pmid17625951-27

A popular medical case, studied in particular by neuroscientist

Antonio Damasio

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio\_Damasio

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Damasio\_1994-28

was that of American railroad worker

Phineas Gage

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phineas\_Gage

. On the 13th of September 1848, while working on a railway track in

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vermont

, he was involved in an accident: an "iron rod used to cram down the explosive powder shot into Gage's cheek, went through the front of his brain, and exited via the top of his head".

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Edmonds\_2014-29

Surprisingly, not only Gage survived, but he also went back to his normal life just in less than two months.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Damasio\_1994-28

Although his physical capacities were restored, however, his personality and his character radically changed. He became vulgar and anti-social: "Where he had once been responsible and self-controlled, now he was impulsive, capricious, and unreliable".

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Edmonds\_2014-29

Damasio wrote: "Gage was no longer Gage."

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Damasio\_1994-28

Moreover, also his moral intuitions were transformed. Further studies by means of

neuroimaging

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neuroimaging

showed a correlation between such "moral" and character transformations and injuries to the ventromedial prefrontal cortex.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-30

In his book

Descartes' Error

, commenting on Phineas Gage case, Damasio said that after the accident the railroad worker was able "To know, but not to feel."

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Damasio\_1994-28

As explained by

David Edmonds

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David\_Edmonds\_(philosopher)

, Joshua Greene thought that this could explain the difference in moral intuitions in different versions of the trolley problem: "We

that we shouldn't push the fat man. But we

it better to save five rather than one life. And the feeling and the thought are distinct."

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Edmonds\_2014-29

Reaction times

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=7

Another critical piece of evidence supporting the dual process account comes from reaction time data associated with moral dilemma experiments. Subjects who choose the "utilitarian" path in moral dilemmas showed increased reaction times under high

cognitive load

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive\_load

in "personal" dilemmas, while those choosing the "deontological" path remained unaffected.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-pmid18158145-31

Cognitive load, in general, is also found to increase the likelihood of "deontological" judgment

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-pmid22698994-32

These laboratory findings are supplemented by work that looks at the decision-making processes of real-world altruists in life-or-death situations.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-pmid25333876-33

These heroes overwhelming described their actions as fast, intuitive, and virtually never as carefully reasoned.

Evolutionary rationale

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=8

dual process theory

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory

is often given an evolutionary rationale (in this basic sense, the theory is an example of

evolutionary psychology

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary\_psychology

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darwinism

thinking, such as

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David\_Hume

Treatise of Human Nature

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A\_Treatise\_of\_Human\_Nature

' we find speculations about the origins of

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morality

as deriving from natural phenomena common to all humans. For instance, he mentions the "common or natural cause of our passions" and the generation of love for others represented through self-sacrifice for the greater good of the group. Hume's work is sometimes cited as an inspiration for contemporary dual process theories.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Singer\_2005-8

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles\_Darwin

evolutionary theory

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution

gives a better descriptive process for how these moral norms are derived from evolutionary processes and natural selection.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Singer\_2005-8

For example, selective pressures favour self-sacrifice for the benefit of the group and punish those who do not. This provides a better explanation of the cost-benefit ratio for the generation of love for others as originally mentioned by

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David\_Hume

Another example of an evolutionarily derived

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social\_norm

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice

, which is born out of the ability to detect those who cheat.

Peter Singer

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter\_Singer

explains justice from the evolutionary perspective by stating the instinct of reciprocity improved fitness for survival, therefore those who did not reciprocate were considered cheaters and cast-off from the group.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Singer\_2005-8

Peter Singer agrees with Greene that

consequentialist

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consequentialism

judgements are to be favored over

deontological

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deontological\_ethics

judgements. According to him, moral constructivism searches for reasonable grounds whereas deontological judgements rely on hasty and emotional responses.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Singer\_2005-8

failedverification

Singer argues our most immediate moral intuitions should be challenged. A normative ethic must not be evaluated by the extent to which it matches those moral intuitions. He gives the example of a brother and sister who secretly decide to have sex with each other using

contraceptives

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth\_control

. Our first intuitive reaction is a firm condemnation of

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incest

as morally wrong. However, a consequentialist judgement brings another conclusion. As the brother and sister did not tell anyone and they used contraceptives, the incest did not have any harmful consequences. Thus, in that case, incest is not necessarily wrong.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Singer\_2005-8

Singer relies on evolutionary theories to justify his claim. For most of our evolutionary history, human beings have lived in small groups where violence was ubiquitous. Deontological judgements linked to emotional and intuitive responses were developed by human beings as they were confronted with personal and close interactions with others. In the past century, our social organizations were altered and so these types of interactions have become less frequent. Therefore, Singer argues that we should rely on more sophisticated consequentialist judgements that fit better in our modern times than the deontological judgements that were useful for more rudimentary interactions.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Singer\_2005-8

Scientific criticisms

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=9

Extensive form

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extensive\_form\_game

representation of a two proposal ultimatum game. Player 1 can offer a fair (F) or unfair (U) proposal; player 2 can accept (A) or reject (R).

Several scientific criticisms have been leveled against the dual process account. One asserts that the dual emotional/rational

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific\_modelling

ignores the motivational aspect of decision making in human social contexts.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-pmid18400930-34

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-35

A more specific example of this criticism focuses on the ventromedial prefrontal cortex lesion data. Although patients with this damage display characteristically "cold-blooded" behaviour in the trolley problem, they show more likelihood of endorsement of emotionally laden choices in the

Ultimatum Game

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultimatum\_Game

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-pmid17251437-36

It is argued that moral decisions are better understood as integrating emotional, rational, and motivational information, the last of which has been shown to involve areas of the brain in the

limbic system

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limbic\_system

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brainstem

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-37

Methodological Worries

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=10

Other criticisms focus on the methodology of using moral dilemmas such as the trolley problem. These criticisms note the lack of affective realism in contrived moral dilemmas and their tendency to use the actions of strangers to offer a view of human moral sentiments.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul\_Bloom\_(psychologist)

, in particular, has argued that a multitude of attitudes towards the agents involved is important in evaluating an individual's moral stance, as well as evaluating the motivations that may inform those decisions.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-38

Kahane and Shackel scrutinize the questions and dilemmas Greene et al. use, and claim that the methodology used in the neuroscientific study of intuitions needs to be improved.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-39

However, after Kahane and colleagues engineered a set of moral dilemmas specifically meant to falsify Greene's theory, their moral dilemmas turned out to confirm it instead.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2014-6

Berker has raised three methodological worries about Greene's empirical findings.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Berker\_2009-9

First It is not the case that only deontological judgements are tied to cognitive processes. In fact, one region of the brain traditionally associated with the emotions –

the posterior cingulate

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Posterior\_cingulate\_cortex

– appears to be activated for characteristically consequentialist judgements. While it is not clear how crucial a role this region plays in moral judgements, one can argue that all moral judgements seem to involve at least some emotional processing. This would disprove the simplest version of the dual-process hypothesis. Greene responded to this argument by proposing that emotions that drive deontological judgements are "alarmlike", whereas those that are present during consequentialist judgements are "more like currency."

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-40

A response which Berker regards to be without empirical backing.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Berker\_2009-9

Berker's second methodological worry is that Greene et al. presented the response-time data to moral dilemmas in a statistically invalid way. Rather than calculating the average difference in response time between the "appropriate" responses and "inappropriate" responses for

moral dilemma, Greene et al. calculated the average response time of the

"appropriate" and the

"inappropriate" responses. Because of this way of calculating, the differences from question to question significantly skewed the results, Berker points out that some questions involved "easy" cases that should not be classified as dilemmas. This is because of the way these cases were framed, people found one of the choices to be obviously inappropriate.

Third, Berker argues Greene's criteria to classify impersonal and personal moral dilemmas do not map onto the distinction of deontological and consequentialist moral judgements. It is not the case consequentialist judgements only arise if cases involve impersonal factors. Berkner highlights the "Lazy Susan Case", where the only way to save five people who sit on a Lazy Susan is to push the lazy Susan into an innocent bystander, killing him. so that serves as a counter-example. Although this

thought experiment

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thought\_experiment

involves personal harm, the philosopher Francis Kamm arrives at an intuitive consequentialist judgement, thinking it is permissible to kill one to save five.

Notwithstanding the above, the later criticism has been considered by Greene.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-41

More recent methodological concerns stem from new evidence suggesting that deontological inclinations are not necessarily more emotional or less rational than utilitarian inclinations. For example, cognitive reflection predicts

utilitarian and deontological inclinations,

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-42

but only by dissociating these moral inclinations with a more advanced protocol that was not used in early dual process theoretic research.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-43

Further, there is evidence that utilitarian decisions are associated with more emotional regret than deontological decisions.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-44

Evidence like this complicates dual process theorists' claims that utilitarian thinking is more rational or that deontological thinking is more emotional.

Failure of the non-negotiability hypothesis

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=11

Guzmán, Barbato, Sznycer, and

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leda\_Cosmides

pointed out that moral dilemmas were a recurrent adaptive problem for ancestral humans, whose social life created multiple responsibilities to others (siblings, parents and offspring, cooperative partners, coalitional allies, and so on). Intermediate solutions, ones that strike a balance between conflicting moral values, would have often promoted fitness better than neglecting one value to fully satisfy others. A capacity to make intermediate or "compromise" judgments would have been favored by natural selection.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:5-45

The dual process model, however, rules out the possibility of moral compromises. According to Greene and colleagues, people experience the footbridge problem as a dilemma because "two \[dissociable psychological\] processes yield different answers to the same question".

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:6-46

On the one hand, System 2 outputs a utilitarian judgment: "push the bystander." On the other hand, System 1 activates an "alarm bell" emotion: "do not harm the bystander." Greene and colleagues claim that the prohibition to harm is "nonnegotiable": It cannot be weighed against other values, including utilitarian considerations.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2008-5

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:6-46

They argue that "\[i\]ntractable dilemmas arise when psychological systems produce outputs that are... non-negotiable because their outputs are processed as absolute demands, rather than fungible preferences."

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:6-46

The dual-process model predicts that subtle changes in context will cause people to flip between extreme judgments: deontic and utilitarian. Moral compromises will be infrequent "trembling hand mistakes."

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:5-45

Trolley problems cannot be used to test for non-negotiability, because they force extreme responses (e.g., push or do not push).

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:5-45

So, to test the prediction, Guzmán and colleagues designed a sacrificial moral dilemma that permits compromise judgments, of the form "sacrifice

people to save

> 1 people are saved for each one sacrificed. Their experimental results show that people make many compromise judgments, which respect the axioms of rational choice. These results contradict the dual-process model, which claims that only deontic judgments are the product of moral rationality. The results indicate the existence of a moral tradeoff system that weighs competing moral considerations and finds a solution that is most right, which can be a compromise judgment.

Alleged ethical implications

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=12

Greene ties the two processes to two existing classes of ethical theories in moral philosophy.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2008-5

He argues that the existing tension between

deontological theories

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deontological\_ethics

of ethics that focus on "right action" and

utilitarian theories

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utilitarianism

that focus on "best results" can be explained by the dual-process organisation of the human mind. Ethical decisions that fall under 'right action' correspond to automatic-emotional (system 1) processing, whereas 'best results' correspond to conscious-controlled reasoning (system 2).

One illustration of this tension are intuitions about

trolley-cases

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trolley\_problem

that differ across the dimension of personal force.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2014-6

When people are asked whether it would be right or wrong to flick a switch in order to divert a trolley from killing five people, their intuitions usually indicate that flicking the switch is the morally right choice. However, when the same scenario is presented to people, but instead of flicking a switch, subjects are asked whether they would push a fat man onto the rails in order to stop the trolley, intuitions usually have it that pushing the fat man is the wrong choice. Given that both actions lead to the saving of five people, why is one judged to be right, whereas the other one wrong? According to Greene, there is no moral justification for this difference of intuitions between the 'switch' and the 'fat man' trolley-case. Instead, what leads to such difference is the morally irrelevant fact that the 'fat man' case involves the use of personal force (thus leading most people to judge that pushing the fat man is the wrong action), whereas the 'switch' case doesn't (thus leading most people to judge that flicking the switch is the right action).

Greene takes such observations as point of departure to argue that judgments produced by automatic-emotional processes lack normative force in comparison to those produced by conscious-controlled processes. Relying on automatic, emotional responses when dealing with unfamiliar moral dilemmas would mean to bank on "

cognitive miracles"

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2014-6

Greene subsequently proposes that this vindicates consequentialism. He rejects deontology as a moral framework as holds that deontological theories may be reduced to "post-hoc" rationalisations of arbitrary emotional responses.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2008-5

Greene's "direct route"

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=13

Greene firstly argues that scientific findings can help us reach interesting

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normative

conclusions, without crossing the

is–ought gap

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Is–ought\_problem

. For example, he considers the normative statement "capital juries make good judgements". Scientific findings could lead us to revise this judgement if it were found that capital juries were, in fact, sensitive to race if we accept the uncontroversial normative

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premise

that capital juries ought not to be sensitive to race.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2014-6

Greene then states that the evidence for dual-process theory might give us reason to question judgements which are based upon moral intuitions, in cases where those moral intuitions might be based upon morally irrelevant factors. He gives the example of incestuous siblings. Intuition might tell us that this is morally wrong, but Greene suggests that this intuition is the result of incest historically being evolutionary disadvantageous. However, if the siblings take extreme precautions, such as

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasectomy

, in order to avoid the risk of genetic mutation in their offspring, the cause of the moral intuition is no longer relevant. In such cases, scientific findings have given us reason to ignore some of our moral intuitions, and in turn revise the moral judgements which are based upon these intuitions.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2014-6

Greene's "indirect route"

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=14

Greene is not making the claim that moral judgements based on emotion are categorically bad. His position is that the different "settings" are appropriate for different scenarios.

With regards to automatic settings, Greene says we should only rely on these when faced with a moral problem that is sufficiently "familiar" to us. Familiarity, on Greene's conception, can arise from three sources - evolutionary history,

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture

and personal experience. It is possible that fear of snakes, for instance, can be traced to genetic dispositions, whereas a reluctance to place one's hand on a stove is caused by previous experience on burning one's hand on a hot stove.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2014-6

The appropriateness of applying our intuitive and automatic mode of reasoning to a given moral problem thus hinges on how the process was formed in the first place. Shaped by trial-and-error experience, automatic settings will only function well when one has sufficient experience of the situation at hand.

In light of these considerations, Greene formulates the "

No Cognitive Miracles Principle

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2014-6

When we are dealing with unfamiliar\* moral problems, we ought to rely less on automatic settings (automatic emotional responses) and more on manual mode (conscious, controlled reasoning), lest we bank on cognitive miracles.

This has implications for philosophical discussion of what Greene calls "unfamiliar problems", or ethical problems ones with which we have inadequate evolutionary, cultural, or personal experience. We might have to attentively revise our intuitions for topics like

climate change

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate\_change

genetic engineering

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic\_engineering

, global terrorism, global poverty, etc. As Greene states, this doesn't mean that our intuitions will always be wrong, but it means we need to pay attention as to where they come from and how they fare compared to more rational argument.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Greene\_2014-6

Philosophical criticisms

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=15

Greene's 2008 article "The Secret Joke of Kant's Soul"

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-47

argues that Kantian/deontological ethics tends to be driven by emotional respondes and is best understood as rationalization rather than rationalism—an attempt to justify intuitive moral judgments post-hoc, although the author states that his argument is speculative and will not be conclusive. Several philosophers have written critical responses, mainly criticising the necessary linking between process, automatic or controlled, intuitive or counterintuitive/rational, with containt, respectively, deontological or utilitarian.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-48

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-49

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-50

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-51

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-52

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-53

Thomas Nagel

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas\_Nagel

has argued that Joshua Greene, in his book

Moral Tribes,

is too quick to conclude utilitarianism specifically from the general goal of constructing an impartial morality; for example, he says,

Immanuel Kant

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immanuel\_Kant

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John\_Rawls

offer other impartial approaches to ethical questions.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Nagel2013-54

irrelevantcitation

Robert Wright

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert\_Wright\_(journalist)

has called Joshua Greene's proposal for global harmony ambitious adding, "I like ambition!"

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Wright2013-55

But he also claims that people have a tendency to see facts in a way that serves their

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ingroups\_and\_outgroups

, even if there's no disagreement about the underlying moral principles that govern the disputes. "If indeed we're wired for tribalism," Wright explains, "then maybe much of the problem has less to do with differing moral visions than with the simple fact that my tribe is my tribe and your tribe is your tribe. Both Greene and

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul\_Bloom\_(psychologist)

cite studies in which people were randomly divided into two groups and immediately favored members of their own group in allocating resources -- even when they knew the assignment was random."

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Wright2013-55

Instead, Wright proposes that "nourishing the seeds of enlightenment indigenous to the world's tribes is a better bet than trying to convert all the tribes to utilitarianism -- both more likely to succeed, and more effective if it does."

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Wright2013-55

Berker's criticisms

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=16

In a widely cited critique of Greene's work and the philosophical implications of the dual process theory,

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard\_University

philosophy professor

Selim Berker

https://scholar.harvard.edu/sberker/biocv

critically analyzed four arguments that might be inferred from Greene and Singer's conclusion.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Berker\_2009-9

He labels three of them as merely

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhetoric

or "bad arguments", and the last one as "the argument from irrelevant factors".

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Berker\_2009-9

According to Berker, all of them are

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fallacy

Three bad arguments

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=17

While the three bad arguments identified by Berker are not explicitly made by Greene and Singer, Berker considers them as implicit in their reasoning.

The first is the

"Emotions Bad, Reasoning Good"

argument. According to it, our

deontological

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deontological\_ethics

intuitions are driven by emotions, while

consequentialist

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consequentialism

intuitions imply abstract reasoning. Therefore, deontological intuitions don't have any normative force, whereas consequentialist intuitions do. Berker claims that this is

question begging

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Begging\_the\_question

for two reasons. First, because there is no support for the claim that emotionally driven intuitions are less reliable than those guided by reason. Secondly, because the argument seems to rely on the assumption that deontological intuitions involve

emotional processes whereas consequentialist intuitions involve

abstract reasoning. For Berker, this assumption also lacks

empirical evidence

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empirical\_evidence

. As a matter of fact, Greene's research

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-pmid15473975-2

itself shows that consequentialist responses to personal moral dilemmas involve at least one brain region - the

posterior cingulate-

that is associated with emotional processes. Hence, the claim that deontological judgements are less reliable than consequentialist judgements because they are influenced by emotions cannot be justified.

The second bad argument presented by Berker is

"The Argument from Heuristics"

, which is an improved version of the "Emotions Bad, Reasoning Good" argument. It is claimed that emotion-driven processes tend to involve fast heuristics, which makes them unreliable. It follows that deontological intuitions, being an emotional form or reasoning themselves, should not be trusted. According to Berker this line of thought is also flawed. This is so because forms of reasoning that consist in heuristics are usually those in which we have a clear notion of what is right and wrong. Hence, in the moral domain, where these notions are highly disputed, "it is question begging to assume that the emotional processes underwriting deontological intuitions consist in heuristics".

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Berker\_2009-9

Berker also challenges the very assumption that heuristics lead to unreliable judgements. Additionally, he argues that, as far as we know, consequentialist judgements may also rely on heuristics, given that it is highly unlikely that they could always be the product of accurate and comprehensive mental calculations of all the possible outcomes.

The third bad argument is

"The Argument from Evolutionary History".

It draws on the idea that our different moral responses towards personal and impersonal harms are evolutionarily based. In fact, since personal violence has been known since ancient age, human developed emotional responses as innate

alarm systems

in order to adapt, handle and promptly respond to such situations of violence within their groups. Cases of impersonal violence, instead, do not raise the same innate alarm and therefore they leave room for more accurate and analytical judgement of the situation. Thus, according to this argument, unlike consequentialist intuitions, emotion-based deontological intuitions are the side effects of this evolutionary adaption to the pre-existing environment. Therefore, "deontological intuitions, unlike consequentialist intuitions, do not have any normative force".

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Berker\_2009-9

Berker states that this is an incorrect conclusion because there is no reason to think that consequentialist intuitions are not also by-products of evolution.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Berker\_2009-9

Moreover, he argues that the invitation, advanced by Singer,

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Singer\_2005-8

to separate evolutionary-based moral judgements (allegedly unreliable) from those that are based on reason, is misleading because it is based on a

false dichotomy

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False\_dilemma

The Argument from Morally Irrelevant Factors

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=18

Berker argued that the most promising argument from neural "is" to moral "ought" is the following.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Berker\_2009-9

"P1. The emotional processing that gives rise to deontological intuitions responds to factors that make a dilemma personal rather than impersonal.

P2. The factors that make a dilemma personal rather than impersonal are morally irrelevant.

C1. So, the emotional processing that gives rise to deontological intuitions responds to factors that are morally irrelevant.

C2. So, deontological intuitions, unlike consequentialist intuitions, do not have any genuine normative force."

Berker criticises both premises and the move from C1 to C2. Regarding P1, Berker is not convinced that deontological judgments are correctly characterized as merely appealing to factors that make the dilemma personal. For example, Kamm's

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-56

'Lazy Susan' trolley case is an example of a 'personal' dilemma which elicits a characteristically

consequentialist

response. Regarding P2, he argues that factors that make a dilemma personal or impersonal are not necessarily morally irrelevant. Moreover, he adds, P2 is 'armchair philosophizing': it cannot be deduced from neuroscientific results that the closeness of a dilemma is bears on its

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-Berker\_2009-9

Eventually, Berker concludes that even if we accept P1 and P2, C1 doesn't necessarily entail C2. This is because it may be the case that consequentialist intuitions

respond to morally irrelevant factors. Unless we can show that this is not the case, the inference from C1 to C2 is invalid.

Intuition as wisdom

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=19

Many philosophers appeal to what is colloquially known as the

yuck factor

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuck\_factor

, or the belief that a widespread common negative intuition towards something is evidence that there is something morally wrong about it. This opposes Greene's conclusion that intuitions should not be expected to "perform well" or give us good ethical reasoning for some ethical problems.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leon\_Kass

Wisdom of Repugnance

presents a prime example of a feelings-based response to an

ethical dilemma

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethical\_dilemma

. Kass attempts to make a case against human cloning on the basis of the widespread strong feelings of repugnance at cloning. He lists examples of the various unpalatable consequences of cloning and appeals to notions of

human nature

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human\_nature

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dignity

to show that our disgust is the emotional expression of deep wisdom that is not fully articulable.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-57

There is a widespread debate on the role of

moral emotions

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral\_emotions

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guilt\_(emotion)

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empathy

and their role in philosophy, and intuitions' relationship to them.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-58

The role of empathy

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=20

In particular, the role of empathy in morality has recently been heavily criticized by commentators such as

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesse\_Prinz

, who describes it as "prone to biases that render moral judgment potentially harmful."

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-59

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul\_Bloom\_(psychologist)

, author of

'Against Empathy : The Case for Rational Compassion'

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Against\_Empathy

labels empathy as

"narrow-minded, parochial, and innumerate",

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-60

primarily due to the deleterious effects that can arise when entrusting emotional, un-reasoned responses to tackling complex ethical issues, which can only be adequately addressed via rationality and reflection.

An example of this is

'The Identifiable victim effect'

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identifiable\_victim\_effect

, where subjects exhibit a much stronger emotional reaction to the suffering of a

victim, as opposed to the weaker emotional response experienced when responding to the suffering of a large-scale, anonymous group (even though the benefit conferred by the subject would be of equal utility in both cases).

This exemplifies the potential for empathy to 'misfire' and motivates the widely shared consensus in the

moral enhancement

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral\_enhancement

debate that more is required than the amplification of certain emotions. Increasing an agent's empathy by artificially raising

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxytocin

levels will likely be ineffective in improving their overall moral agency, because such a disposition relies heavily on psychological, social and situational contexts, as well as their deeply held convictions and beliefs.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:1-61

it is likely that augmenting higher-order capacities to

one's moral responses in a flexible, reason-sensitive, and context-dependent way would be a more reliable, and in most cases more desirable, means to agential moral enhancement.

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)#cite\_note-:1-61

https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Dual\_process\_theory\_(moral\_psychology)&action=edit&section=21

# In Search of Morality: An Interview with Dr. Joshua Greene - Brain World Magazine

In Search of Morality: An Interview with Dr. Joshua Greene | Brain World

In Search of Morality: An Interview with Dr. Joshua Greene

https://brainworldmagazine.com/category/arts/

https://brainworldmagazine.com/category/education/

https://brainworldmagazine.com/category/people/

https://brainworldmagazine.com/category/reviews/

https://brainworldmagazine.com/category/science/

https://brainworldmagazine.com/category/all/

Published on November 2, 2019

BW: Is this what is referred to as the “dual-process” theory of moral judgment?

Yes. The dual-process theory of moral judgment says that moral judgments are influenced by automatic emotional intuitions, on the one hand, and more detached, rule-based reasoning on the other.

If you're like most people, when you hear about the footbridge dilemma you have both reactions competing in your head. You have detached, cost-benefit thinking telling you it makes sense to push, and you have an automatic emotional response telling you that this is wrong. You can see this in the brain imaging studies, but you can also see it in more low-tech experiments. If you put people under time pressure, for example, they are more likely to say it's wrong to push the guy off the footbridge.

The dual-process theory says that people have mixed responses. But which response is right? That's an even more complicated — and controversial — issue. In general, I think it's good we are reluctant to push people off footbridges. It's good to have a strong immediate negative emotional response to committing an act of violence. It's certainly not something we would want to get rid of.

But, any emotional response has its limitations. These moral dilemmas create a situation in which a generally good gut reaction favors a worse outcome. It may be that, here, our gut reaction is wrong, but it's wrong in a generally good way.

My preferred analogy for the dual-process theory is a digital SLR camera. You've got your automatic settings (“landscape,” “portrait”) and your manual mode. Which way of taking photos is better? Neither is absolutely better. The manual and automatic settings are good for different things. If you're doing something standard, with pretty typical goals in mind, point-and-shoot settings are probably better. But if you're trying to do something fancy, something the manufacturer of the camera didn't envision, then you want to put the camera in manual mode.

Automatic settings are like our gut reactions, while manual mode is deliberate reasoning. I would say that for everyday moral problems, you want to trust your moral intuitions, which tell you things like, “don't lie and don't steal, and don't do all of those generally bad things.” But when you're facing peculiarly modern moral problems — for example, bioethical dilemmas like abortion and physician-assisted suicide — you should be skeptical of your gut reactions and rely more on reasoning.

BW: What you just said rings true of personal relationships as well.

Yes, I think there's something to that. Counseling psychologists say to express your disagreements with “I” statements. Instead of “you did this,” or “you shouldn't have done that,” describe where your behavior and feelings are coming from. You lay your facts on the table and resist the temptation to draw conclusions for as long as possible.

I think this approach may also be effective when it comes to disagreements between groups. When we talk about “rights,” it is, I think, really just a way of insisting our gut reactions are correct. If we can take a step back and simply describe where we're coming from, and what our concerns and values are, that might be more effective than making high-minded declarations about who has what rights.

BW: Have you ever found that men or women tend to do one more than the other?

The tendencies that I've seen are general human tendencies, more apparent in some cultures than others, but not tied in any strong way to gender.

There are interesting differences between nations like the U.S. that are individualist and more collectivist ones like India. Research by Joe Henrich and colleagues indicates that many cultural differences stem from how people earn a living. In “Moral Tribes,” I discuss how in smaller societies where people hunt on their own, they don't expect much from other people, and they don't give much themselves. In small-scale societies where people need elaborate cooperation to survive — doing things like whale hunting, for example — people tend to be much more generous.

BW: What about moral decision-making and money?

We've run many studies with financial stakes. In one set of experiments conducted with David Rand and Martin Nowak, we had people play the “public goods game.” Each participant gets $10. They can decide whether to keep their money, put all their money, or just a portion into a common pool. The money put in gets doubled and is equally divided among the four people. If you're selfish, you simply keep your money and get a share of whatever the other people put in. That's how you maximize your own payoff. But, if you care about the group as a whole, you put all your money into the pool.

In one of these experiments, we put people under time pressure. If your gut reaction is to be cooperative, then time pressure should make you give more, and having time to think, “Well, maybe I shouldn't. I'll lose my money,” will make you give less. By contrast, if your first instinct is to be selfish, you may initially think, “I want to keep my money,” but then after deliberating you might say, “Well, maybe I should put the money in the pool for the greater good.” A third possibility is that there are no competing impulses. People will just do what they wholeheartedly want to do. We don't see this in all populations, but at least in some people, the more automatic reaction is to be cooperative, and further thinking makes people less likely to contribute.

BW: Would you say that those who make intuitively unappealing choices tend to have longer reaction times than those who make emotionally pleasant decisions?

I think that, in general, people have prosocial emotions. This is the core of morality.

We have negative and positive social emotions. We can apply them to our own behavior and we can apply them to the behavior of other people. If I care about the person I'm interacting with — someone I love, a friend, or just a stranger towards whom I have good will, my gut tells me to be cooperative, and doing that is rewarding. Likewise, I'll feel guilty if I'm not cooperative.

We apply this thinking to others as well. If \[in the public goods game\] you put your money in, you'll have the gratitude of the others who benefit. If you don't, then you'll have their contempt and their scorn. These social emotions motivate us to be cooperative — to think about other people's well-being instead of just our own. Fortunately, we live in a society in which being exceptionally selfish is also shortsighted. Here, anti-social people tend not to do as well in the long run. But there are places where if you're cooperative and trusting, you'll loose.

This article was first published in Brain World Magazine's Fall 2014 issue.

> Joshua Greene: Moral Tribes

Joshua Greene: Moral Tribes

10 Avg 2025

https://principus.si/2025/08/10/

https://principus.si/author/principus/

https://principus.si/2025/08/10/joshua-greene-moral-tribes/#respond
